•seventy four only) for import of (1) Long pepper (Pippall)- 
9-49 MT-Rs, 4,22,494/(2) Ehied seed grapes (Munnakc)- 
33.25 MT.-Rg. 7,26,180^. Under G-C.A,

^oth copies) of tho above mentioned licence on the ground 
mat the original licence has been lost or misplac^. It has

and affidavit on stamped paper duly sworn in before u 
Notary Public Delhi. I am accordingly satisfletl that the 
original import licence No. P1D12279224 dated 6-2-90 has 
been lost or misplaced by the firm. In cxcercise of the 
powers conferred under sub-clause 9(cc) of the Import 
(Control) Order, 1955 dated 7-12-1955 as amended the said

MINISTRY OF ENERGY 
(Deptt. of Coal) 

New Delhi, the 24th April, 1990 

S.O. 1412.—^Whereas it appears to the Central Government that coal is likely to be obtained from the 
lands mentioned in the Schedule hereto annexed; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub-section (1) of scction-4 of the Coal Bearing 
Areas (Acquisition and Development) Act, 1957 (20 of 1957), the Central Government hereby gives notice 
of its intention to prospect for coal ther-ein. 

The plan of the area covered by this notification may be inspected in the OlEce of the Collector, 
Burdwan (West Bengal) or in the Office of the Coal Controller, 1-Council House Street, Calcutta or in the 
Office of the Director (Tech.) Past. Eastern Coalfields Ltd,, Sanctoria, P.O. Disergarh, Burdwan (West 
Bengal). 

Any person interested in the lands covered by this notification shall deliver all maps, charts and other 
documents referred to in sub-section 7 of section 13 of the said Act to the Director (Tech,) East, Eastern 
Coalfields Ltd., Sanctoria, within NINETY days from the date of the publication of this notification in the 
Gazette of India.

By Order No. L-19012(38)/84-D.lV(B) dated 13-2-1985 the 
Government of India, Ministry erf Labour referred the follow¬ 
ing dispute to this Tribunal for adjudication : 

“Whether the managenienl of Toposi Colliciy of Mev:is 
Eastern Coalfields Limited, P.O. Toposi, Distt. 
Burdwan (\VB), is justified in dismissing Smt. Dasi 
Bouri, Wagon Loader with elfect from 29-10-83 ? If 
not to what relief tlie workman is entitled ?’ 

2. When the case is called out today, Mrs. R. Rathore, 
Deputy Personnel Manager of the Colliery appears for the 
employer. Nobody appears for the Union. A petition has how¬ 
ever been received from the Union stating tlierein that the 
Union is not interested to proceed with the present refer^rticc 
and the Union has prayed tor a “No Dispute Award”. 
Mrs, Rathore appearing for the employer has no objection 
.in this regard. 

3. On due consideration of the petition of the Union as 
well as the submission of Mrs. Rathore appearing on behalf 
of the employer, I find that this Tribunal has no other alter¬ 
native but to pass ft “No Dispute Award" and accordingly a 
‘No Dispute Award' is passed. 

This is my Award. 

Dated, Calcutta, 

The 10th April, 1990

AWARD 

By Order No. L-190l2(53)y86-D.IV(B) doted 23rd January 
1987, the Government of India, Ministry of Labour referred 
the following dispute to this Tribunal for adjudication : 

“Whether the action of the Management of Victoria West 
Colliery of M/s. BCCL P.O. Barakar, Dist. Burdwaii 
in not referring Shri Sunder Barhi, Surface Pump 
Operator to the Medical Board inspite of the request 
made by him to this effect on account of his suffering 
from disease of permanent nature, i.c. T.B. and 
depriving him of I lie benefit of Clause 10,4.3 of 
NCWA JI is jiLstified ? ]f not, to what relief the 
workman concerned is entitled ?” 

2. When the case h called out today, Mr, S. Banerjee, 
Advocate appears for the employer and filers a Joint Petition 
of compromise, duly signed by both parties- He prays for 
an Award in terms of the 3omt Petition of Compromise. 
Considered the said Joint Petition of Compromise as well os 
the submission of Mr. Banerjc^. The terms of Joint Petition 
of Compromise appear to be fair, reasonable and in the 
interest of the partr^'s. I therefore, accept the same and pass 
an 'Award’ in terms of the said Joint Petition of Compromise 
which do form part of this Award as Annexure-A. 

This is my Award, 

Dated, Calcutta, 

The 12th April, 1990. 

SUKUMAR CHAKRAVARTY. Presiding Officer

not lecnver the monev from the petitioner. The respondenf- 
would contend that this is net a dispute coming under the 


definition of Section 2(k) of the industrial Disputes Act. Sec¬ 
tion 2(k) say's that “Industrial DRpute" means any 
disrate or Uiffeieiice between employers and employers, 
or between cmploycix> and workmen, or between workmen 
and woikmcn. whic/i is cunnerfed with the employment or 
noji-cniplo;/ment or ihe terms of employment or wdh the 
conditions of laboui, of any pe.Non.’* The refll^al to pay

said to a dispute or a difference between the employer and 
ihe employee m this ca^e. It cannot therefore he said that 
there is no Industrial Dispute us defined in Section 2(k) of 
the Industiial Disputes Act and therefore 1 am of .the view 
that Ihe reference is not bad.

APPEARANCE : 

Order No. L-40012/108/88-D. 11 (B), dated 7-8-1989 of 
the Ministry of labour. Government of India, New 
Delhi. 

This dispute coming on this day for final disposal u^n 
perusing the reference, claim statement and all other material 
papers on record and upon hearing the arguments of Thiru 
K. Jay^iaman, Advocate appearing for the workman and the 
Management being absent, this Tribunal passed the fdllbw 
ing 

AWARD 

This dispute between the workman and the management 
of Madras Telephones, Madras arises out of a reference 
under section lCKl)(d) of the Industrial Disputes Acu 1947, 
by the Government of India in its Order No. L-40012/108/ 
88-D. II (B), dated 7lh August, 1989 of the Ministry of 
Labour for adjudication of the following issue :— 

“Whether the Management of Madras Telephones is 
iustifled in tcrmmatftig the service of Shri B. 
Anthony, casual labour with effect from 2242- 
1987 7 If not, to whnt relief the worker concerned 
is entitled 7”. 

2. Parties were served with summons, 

3. Petitioner-worker^ filed claim statement on 9-12-1989 
utting forth his claim. Petitioner-worker was represented 
y counsel. 

4. In spite of several adjournments, the management was 
not represented and no counter statement wa.s filed. Hence 
the management was set exparte and the matter was posted 
to 16-4-1990 for enquiry, 

5. To-day the petitioner-worker was represented by counsel 
and examined himself as W.W. 1. Ex. W.l to W. ll were 
marked through him. Claim proved. Heard the counsel for 
petitioner-worker. 

6. An award is .passed directing the management to re¬ 
instate the petitioner-worker with continuity of service, full 
back wages and hll other attendant benefits as prayed for 
in his claim statement. No costs. 

Dated, this 16th day of April, 1990. 

N. PALANI APPAN, Industrial Tribunal

arc ctitled to no relief-

-5. 4 he first questioii that fads for decision is whether

0. As regards the nature of control, ihe evidence on 
record clearly shows that the Workman was under e.xclusivc 
and absolute control of the Bank. As the papers and the 
Workman’s oral evidence show, his prime job was to see 
that pledged goods were not released to the factory con¬ 
cerned without the express sanction of the Branch Manager 
of the Bank. I will just refer to a letter dated 22-8-198K 
(Ex. W-2) addressed by the Agent of the Takii Sikandcr 
Bank Branch directly to the Workman, icquiring him to 
release certain amounts of sugar to the factory. In compli¬ 
ance with this order, the Workman released specified quan¬ 
tities of goods after obtaining the acknowledgement of the 
rcpre^itaitvc of the sugar tuclory. There is' no matermi 
v^hether to think even for a moment lhat the Workman 
ever took orders from the factory. Indeed his exclusive jcb 
was lo see lhat the interests of the Bank ever remained 
eficctivcly secured and guarded against the factory con- 
ceined, in that it did not lift any sugar imauthorisedly. 
The salary of the Workman no doubt was ultimately 
debited to. the sugar factory. However, it is crystal that in 
the first instance, it was the Bank who paid it lo him, 
obviously because he was their servant. Tt does not at all 
matter, if under the mutual arrangement between the Bank 
and the factory, the latter has to bear the expenditure on 
this count. Taking into consideiation the entire evidence 
on iccord, documentary as well as oral, the epuclusion is 
inescapable that the Workman was under the exclusive and 
absolute control of the Bank and was paid his salary by 
them. I accordingly hold that he has all along been in emp¬ 
loyee of the Bank. 

The next question tluit now arises is whether the Work¬ 
man is entitled to the wages and other benefits as available 
to the clerical grade under the Bipartite Settlements. He 
has adduced some evidence to show tiiat- apart from God¬ 
own Keeper’s work he was also doing some work which 
is normally done by clerks. In particular reference may be 
made lo a iuint report filed by the two sides (Ex, 
t>n orders of my learned predecessor Shri Justice M.S, 
Jamdar. However, in the course of the arguments the 
Workmen ‘Union's learned advocate Shri Vaidya has made 
a statement that they would be satisfied, if the Workman 
is allowed the benefits available to a Godown Keeper only. 
The scope of my deliberations stands restricted accordingly. 

H. The first objection of the Bank is that Workman’s 
appointment is not accord with the extant rules, and that 
as such he is not entitled to claim the benefits which a 
regular employee can. It is pointed out that at the time 
of bis initial appointment the Workman was overage, and 
that his appointments were made on an ad-hoc basis on 
each of the three occasions, the process having been initi¬ 
aled by none else but himself, by preferring .applications 
to the Divisional Manager of the Bank. T am clear that 
in liiw or equity the Bank cannot take advantage of Us 
own lapse or fault, in order to defeat the Workman’s claim. 
The Workman ha.s w'orked as Godown Keeper and ren¬ 
dered faithful service to the Bank for more than 9 years 
now. The Bank do not appear to have any grievance about 
Ihe qiiniilv of hi, Work. The principle of equal pay for 
c^iial woik will, in my In.ruble opinion^ wlU apply hero 



with full force. The Bank’s main contention appeals to be 
that the Workman accepted the terms and conditions of 
his employment of his own free will and as such he cannot 
now make any grievance that those are far less favourable, 
as compared to a regular Godown Keeper of the Bank. 
In my opinion, such a defence is not open to the Bank. Jf 
any aiUhority is needed, jcfercncc may be made to the 
decision of the Supreme Coinl reported at I fXJ-1986-SC- 
134 Dhirendra Chamoh and another State ol U.P. 


9. Last I may dispose of the submissions of the Bank 

based on paragraph 20.13 of tlie Bipartite Settlements. I 

have already reproduced the text of this provision in para¬ 
graph 4 supra. I do not find anything m this provision 
which supports the stand taken by the Bank. The Bank 
have actually utilised the services of the Workman not 
only as Godown Keeper but to some extent as a full 

fledged clerk also. The question that foe ally needs be ans¬ 
wered, is whether in a situation where the Bank have them¬ 
selves voluntarily made certain appointment in breach of 
their rules, and availed themselves of the Workman’s ser¬ 
vices and labour, can they make an about turn and legiti¬ 
mately refute their liability to compenstate such a wronged 
person on the ground that they are guilty of breaking their 
own rules of recruitment? In my humble opinion—in law 


1 1'j 191 i 1 I 


and equity—the only alls^^Cl Ivd-^ to be an emphatic ‘No. 
I do not think para 20.13 of tiie Bipartite Settlement ap¬ 
plies to a case, where the Bank lias already made the ap¬ 
pointment, may be in breach of the rules, and reaped the 

fruits of the Workman’s labour. Hiis is a clear case^ where 
the Workman shall be entitled lu the wages and ail other 
benefits available to a regular C»5 h1o.v a Koeper ol the Bank 
under the proMsions in the Bipanitc ^e^ilcmcnl^. 

10. The action of the iManagcment of Bank of Maha¬ 
rashtra in denying to the Workman N.Y. Pinjari the pay 

scale, allowances and other benefits from 10-3-1981 avail¬ 

able to a regular Godown Keeper of the Bank is unjusti¬ 
fied. The Management is di’'cclcd to pay to him within a

of arrears of pay, allowances and other benefits due to him 
under the Bipartite Settlements, on the basis that his ap¬

cost> and pay those of Workmen's Union, which 1 quantify 
at Rs. 500!-, Award accordingly.
